# **ChainTorrent MVP**

## Problem Statement

JavaScript dependency distribution runs through a single centralized registry that is an availability chokepoint, a mutable source of truth, and an intermediary between the people who publish packages and the people who consume them. ChainTorrent's MVP replaces that path with a content-addressed swarm, a canonical on-chain identity registry, and transferable access entitlements, so that the distribution and identity model is proven with monetization out of bounds.

**Adoption runs individual developer first, and everything else follows from that.** A developer at a terminal gains cross-project reuse of whatever their machine has already fetched, swarm retrieval on popular packages, and installs that survive a registry outage. Build platforms adopt next and for their own reasons: a service running the same few thousand popular installs continuously, on always-on machines with real bandwidth, holds the ideal cache as a by-product of its own economics, and seeding that cache costs almost nothing once it exists. That makes them natural superseeders rather than participants anyone has to recruit, and it is why no privileged bootstrap host is part of this plan. CI and enterprise adoption arrive as a consequence of that sequence rather than as targets to be won early.

The sequence matters to this workplan because it determines what the MVP measures. Latency is judged against interactive local installs rather than pipeline tolerance, since a developer notices a doubled install where a pipeline does not. Cache reuse is the headline adoption metric, because it is the benefit the adopting population actually experiences. Registry-outage survival remains true throughout and is simply not the lead, since it matters most to the population that adopts last.

The design is specified in `docs/research/cryptography.md` and bounded in `docs/research/MVP Scope.md`. Undetermined decisions are held in the To-Do list below.

The cryptographic construction is specified; its research record is `docs/research/cryptography-research-notebook.md`, and its statement is `docs/research/cryptography-critical-path.md`. A validation harness on both pairing curves produces the sizes, timings, and gas against the configured piece-group size and curve defaults, and nothing waits on it: every node reads the piece-group size from the deployment record and depends only on the code it calls.

## Objectives

Deliver an install path for JavaScript dependencies that survives registry outages, serves popular packages from a peer swarm, and exercises the identity and entitlement pipeline end to end.

Prove distribution and identity. Willingness to pay and seeder compensation are out of bounds and are held in the To-Do list; the transaction flow is proven at a nominal price on assets the project publishes, as a test instrument rather than a monetization step.

Every undetermined decision is carried in the To-Do list, which every workplan that stems from this one inherits.

## Expected Outcome

A developer installs dependencies and receives a working `node_modules` assembled from a local content-addressed store, a peer swarm, or the ingest source as fallback, with canonical identity resolved on-chain and an access entitlement minted to their wallet.

* `[ ]`   `discovery/aggregate` **Discovery family function aggregating the sources it is handed: every source discovers the same key concurrently, the peers the sources return are unioned in source order with a repeated address kept once and no source authoritative, and a source that does not resolve contributes no peers while its error is carried whole beside them**

  * `[ ]`   `objective`
    * `[ ]`   Problem: several discovery sources run for one object at once, and their results overlap, differ, fail, and come from networks that cannot be trusted; one function joins them, so that the result does not depend on which source answers first, an address several sources return is kept once, no source's answer is taken as the answer, and a source that fails is neither hidden nor allowed to fail the others
    * `[ ]`   Sources: SW-02, whose row in `docs/research/MVP Application Requirements.md` requires multiple discovery implementations to run concurrently, union and deduplicate results, and treat no discovery source as authoritative, and whose proof returns overlapping, conflicting, unavailable, and malicious discovery results and verifies deterministic safe aggregation; `docs/research/MVP Scope.md`'s statement that peer discovery resolves through `IPeerDiscoveryAdapter` with multiple adapters active concurrently, results unioned and deduplicated, and no adapter authoritative; the feature spec's statement that overlapping, conflicting, unavailable, and malicious results aggregate deterministically and safely; the technical requirements' peer discovery family row, whose factory owns the aggregation that unions whichever sources resolve and treats none as authoritative, and its file-tree rule that a function a family owns is a module directory addressed `crate/function`; the system architecture's `discovery` row and swarm engine row, where the family-owned aggregation unions and deduplicates peers; the dependency map's `discovery/aggregate` row, which depends on `discovery/admit_peers` and proves SW-02 aggregation under overlapping, conflicting, unavailable, and malicious results; the Swarm transport and seed host milestone's scope for `discovery/aggregate` and its exit that discovery aggregation is deterministic under conflicting and malicious results; the errors topic's rule that every error is surfaced unchanged
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `aggregate` calls `discover` once on every source in `deps.sources`, concurrently, each with the params' deadline and the payload's key and announce port, and returns the peers of the sources that resolved, unioned in the order of `deps.sources`, each address kept once at its first position, each peer exactly as the source that produced it returned it
    * `[ ]`   Functional: the order of the returned peers is the order of `deps.sources` and of each source's own peers, and does not depend on the order in which the sources finish
    * `[ ]`   Functional: a source whose `discover` returns an error contributes no peers, and the result carries one refusal for it, holding the source's declared identifier and the error unchanged, in the order of `deps.sources`; the result is `Ok` whether all, some, or none of the sources resolved
    * `[ ]`   Functional: a source is never preferred; a peer whose address two sources return is kept as the earlier source in `deps.sources` returned it
    * `[ ]`   Non-functional: the function adds no timer, since the deadline is the sources'; it names no vendor, no transport, and no concrete; it constructs no source and selects none, the caller hands the sources it has resolved

    * `[ ]`   Does not admit addresses: a `DiscoveredPeer` holds a `PeerAddress`, which only `PeerAddress::try_new` constructs, so a peer a source returns is already admitted and is not admitted again
    * `[ ]`   Does not decide which source to trust, retry a source, replace a refusal with an empty result, or drop a refusal
    * `[ ]`   Does not inject peers into a transport; the caller passes the peers it aggregates to the transport

* `[ ]`   `storage/redb` **Embedded key-value store concrete over `redb` holding the declared tables of described keys and values, serving read and write transactions whose commit is a durable checkpoint and whose abort, or drop before commit, leaves the prior checkpoint; creates the `adapters/storage` crate and authors the storage family's generic interface, table name and location types, declaration, and mock**

  * `[ ]`   `objective`
    * `[ ]`   Problem: the job engine, the configuration registry, the ciphertext index, the plaintext CAS index, and the local metrics all persist records through one repo-owned store interface, so that each persisted transition is atomic, survives process and machine failure, and is recoverable; a transaction either becomes the next checkpoint whole or leaves the prior checkpoint as the only state; keys and values pass through the encoding family, so no consumer and no vendor library defines a byte layout; bytes read back from the store are untrusted data and are admitted only through the encoding family's decoder; and no consumer names the store library
    * `[ ]`   Sources: XA-03, whose row in `docs/research/MVP Application Requirements.md` requires durable workflows to be idempotent, observable, cancellable where safe, and recoverable after process or machine failure, and whose proof terminates every workflow at persisted transitions; SW-05, whose row in the same document requires ciphertext storage keyed by ciphertext root and separately budgeted, which the ciphertext index realizes over this store; the technical requirements' storage engine family subsystem row, whose factory owns the key-value store interface, tables, transactions, and checkpoints, and its Daemon local stores section, which states that keys and values are under the encoding family's adapter and that no consumer names the store library; the tech stack's durable job store row, which names `storage/redb` over `redb` `4.3.0` and states that ACID transactions give the checkpointing XA-03 and FF-03 require; the system architecture's `storage` adapter row and its Store integration point; the dependency map's `storage/redb` row, which depends on `encoding/factory`, authors the interface with tables, transactions, and checkpoints, the types with their fallible constructors, the declaration, and the family's mock, creates the `adapters/storage` crate, pins `redb`, and proves the store contract under interruption; the Swarm transport and seed host milestone's scope for `storage/redb`; the `encoding/factory` node for the encoder, decoder, and description surface this node consumes
    * `[ ]`   Functional: a store constructed over a location and a set of declared table names holds every declared table, and a transaction opened on it sees the last checkpoint; a read transaction reads, and a write transaction reads, puts, and removes
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `put` encodes the key and the value through the borrowed encoder under the table's descriptions and stores the bytes; `get` encodes the key, reads the stored bytes, and decodes them under the value description as untrusted bytes; `entries` returns every entry of the table in ascending order of the encoded key bytes, each key and value decoded under the table's descriptions; `remove` encodes the key and deletes its entry
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `commit` makes every change of the write transaction durable together, as the next checkpoint, before it returns; `abort`, and a write transaction dropped before `commit`, leave the prior checkpoint as the only state, including after the store is closed and reopened over the same location
    * `[ ]`   Functional: a table whose name is not among the declared names is refused by every concrete with that table named, before any encoding or vendor call
    * `[ ]`   Functional: every concrete declares its storage identifier, its adapter version, and the interface version it implements, readable before any instance exists
    * `[ ]`   Functional: the borrowed encoder's refusals and the description's refusals are carried unchanged, and the vendor's errors are carried unchanged in the concrete's own variants
    * `[ ]`   Non-functional: `redb` is named only inside `adapters/storage/src/redb`; the crate depends on no repository crate but `adapters/encoding`; keys and values reach `redb` only as the bytes the encoder produced and leave it only through the decoder; the location is a value its caller hands, never a literal; the store holds no secret-typed value

  * `[ ]`   `role`
    * `[ ]`   Adapter: the storage family's first concrete, and the first source file that requires the family's generic interface, transaction interfaces, table name and location types, identifier, declaration, and mock, which it authors in the family's `factory` module as its producers
    * `[ ]`   Creates `factory/mod.rs` with the module wiring alone and `factory/provides.rs` with the interface and mock surface alone; the factory function, its types, its interaction spec with the family's contract entries, its unit test, its re-export, and the family's integration test are `storage/factory`'s
    * `[ ]`   Does not define any key or value type or its byte layout; each caller supplies the descriptions of its table's key and value, and the encoding family encodes them
    * `[ ]`   Does not own the table catalogue; the composition hands the declared table names, and each consumer's node names its own tables
    * `[ ]`   Does not enforce quotas, eviction, or retention; the seed host and the CAS own those and use this store for their indexes
    * `[ ]`   Does not carry a commit

  * `[ ]`   `module`
    * `[ ]`   Bounded context: the `adapters/storage` crate's `factory` module, holding the family's adapter and transaction traits, the table name and location types, the table and entry types, the storage identifier, the declaration, the interface version, every method's params, payload, success, error, and return types, and the family's mock concrete; and its private `redb` concrete, holding the adapter and the transactions over `redb`, their constructor params, and their errors

* `[ ]`   `seed-host/owned` **Owned seed host concrete holding root-keyed ciphertext and sidecar objects in a filesystem store with their holding reason, index, and seeding choice kept through the storage family, verifying custody by a Bao challenge over the stored bytes, serving through the transport family, evicting voluntary holdings to a capacity and never an obligated one, and pausing or resuming serving under the host's clock, battery, meter, and CPU conditions; creates the `adapters/seed-host` crate and authors the seed host family's generic interface, holding types, host conditions interface, declaration, and mock**

  * `[ ]`   `objective`
    * `[ ]`   Problem: a participant's swarm contribution is the ciphertext it holds and serves, and that contribution must run independently of any editor or CLI session, survive a restart, be bounded by a budget separate from the plaintext store, keep every obligated object regardless of that budget, report what it holds with the reason it holds it, and honour the owner's seeding controls; every object is addressed by its root, stored once however many transports serve it, and verified on demand by a Bao challenge instead of a report; the index and the controls reach the store and the host only through repo-owned interfaces, so no consumer and no vendor library defines a layout, a byte form, or a clock
    * `[ ]`   Sources: SW-03, SW-05, SW-07, ST-06, and PR-05 in `docs/research/MVP Application Requirements.md`, whose proofs run a host through a restart while another participant retrieves, multi-home one object under varied capacity policies, compare the archive with the host's verified contents, vary each seeding control while seeding and attempt to evict or unseed an obligated object, and fill both stores independently; SW-04's verification of custody by random Bao authentication challenges, whose procedure the family's `held_and_served` realizes for the owned store; SW-06's resumable acquisition without exposing unauthenticated partial data; the technical requirements' seed host family subsystem row, its Daemon local stores `ciphertext_index` table and its Ciphertext store layout, owned mode, section, and the settings catalogue's Storage and Seeding rows; the product requirements' statements that holding reason, quota, and eviction belong to `seed-host/owned`, that obligated holdings are never evicted by plaintext policy, and that every ciphertext and every sidecar is its own object with its own locator; the tech stack's Swarm transport and seed host row; the dependency map's `seed-host/owned` row, which depends on `hashing/factory`, `encoding/factory`, `transport/factory`, and `storage/factory`, authors `ISeedHostAdapter` with held-and-served, take custody, release, capacity and usage, and enumerate, the holding types with the obligated-versus-voluntary distinction and their fallible constructors, the capacity and holding-reason declaration, and the family's mock, creates the `adapters/seed-host` crate, and proves SW-05, SW-03, SW-07, ST-06, and PR-05 separation; the Swarm transport and seed host milestone's scope for `seed-host/owned`; the nodes `hashing/blake3_bao`, `hashing/factory`, `encoding/factory`, `transport/rqbit`, `transport/factory`, `storage/redb`, and `storage/factory` for the surfaces this node consumes
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `take_custody` places an object's bytes under its root in the store, retrieved through the transport family with every piece Bao-verified before it is held, records its kind, length, piece length, locator, holding reason, and seeding choice in the index in one committed transaction, and, when the capacity would be exceeded, first evicts voluntary holdings in the index's ascending root order until the object fits, refusing with the capacity, the use, and the need when evicting every voluntary holding does not suffice and changing nothing
    * `[ ]`   Functional: taking an object that is already held stores no second copy, calls neither the transport nor the filesystem, and keeps the stronger reason of the stored one and the offered one, so an obligation is never lowered by a voluntary request
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `release` stops serving a voluntary holding, deletes its directory, and removes its index entry; an obligated holding is refused by `release`, by eviction, and by a seeding choice of do-not-seed
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `held_and_served` answers for a held root by drawing a chunk index from the caller's uniform and the stored content length, reading the chunk and its authentication path from the stored bytes and outboard, verifying them against the root, and reporting whether the transport is serving the object; a root the index does not hold is refused as not held, and a chunk that fails verification is refused with the verifier's refusal whole
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `reconcile_serving` reads the host's conditions once and, for every held object in ascending root order, serves it when its seeding choice is seed and the family configuration's controls permit under those conditions, and stops serving it otherwise, so a restarted host serves again after one call and each control takes effect on the next call
    * `[ ]`   Functional: the controls permit serving when the battery pause is off or the host is not on battery, the meter pause is off or the connection is not metered, the idle CPU limit is absent or the host's utilization does not exceed it, and the schedule is empty or one window contains the host's local minute of the week
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `capacity_and_usage` reports the configured capacity and the bytes held, in total and per holding reason; `enumerate` reports every held object, ciphertext and sidecar alike, in ascending root order with its length, reason, seeding choice, and locator
    * `[ ]`   Functional: the concrete declares its identifier, the holding reasons it distinguishes, whether it enforces the capacity, its adapter version, and the interface version it implements, readable before any instance exists
    * `[ ]`   Non-functional: the filesystem is touched only inside `adapters/seed-host/src/owned`; the index records pass through the encoding family and are decoded as untrusted bytes; no bandwidth, port, interface, or transfer control is read here, the transport family's configuration carries them; every value the host acts on, the store root, the staging directory, the index table name, the capacity, and each control, is a value its caller hands; no secret-typed value is held

  * `[ ]`   `role`
    * `[ ]`   Adapter: the seed host family's first concrete, and the first source file that requires the family's generic interface, holding types, host conditions interface, configuration, declaration, and mock, which it authors in the family's `factory` module as its producers; the interface of a host condition source is authored here because this is the first file that consumes it
    * `[ ]`   Creates `factory/mod.rs` with the module wiring and the constructors and accessors of the holding, minute, percentage, and schedule types, and `factory/provides.rs` with the interface and mock surface; the factory function, its types, its interaction spec with the family's contract entries, its unit test, its re-export, and the family's integration test are `seed-host/factory`'s
    * `[ ]`   Does not translate roots to an external client's identifiers or ask an external client for custody; `seed-host/rqbit` does
    * `[ ]`   Does not discover peers, choose which objects to take, or decide a holding reason; the caller hands the peers, the object, and the reason
    * `[ ]`   Does not read the clock, the battery, the meter, or the CPU; the host conditions source the caller hands does, and its concrete is not authored here
    * `[ ]`   Does not own the settings catalogue; the composition passes the capacity, the controls, and the directories as typed values
    * `[ ]`   Does not carry a commit, which `seed-host/factory` carries for the milestone

  * `[ ]`   `module`

* `[ ]`   `seed-host/rqbit` **Delegated seed host concrete driving the rqbit application through its HTTP API: an object's info-hash, length, outboard, holding reason, and seeding choice are kept in an index through the storage family, custody is verified by a Bao challenge whose chunk is read from the application with an HTTP range request and checked against the stored outboard's path rather than trusted from the application's report, and serving is paused or resumed in the application under the host's conditions; adds the concrete beside `seed-host/owned` in the `adapters/seed-host` crate**

  * `[ ]`   `objective`
    * `[ ]`   Problem: a participant who already runs the rqbit application should be able to contribute its held ciphertext to the swarm through the same seed host interface as the owned store, and an external application's account of what it holds is never evidence of custody; the object is addressed by its root, the application by the info-hash of the torrent that carries it, and the translation, the holding reason, and the seeding choice live in the host because the application stores none of them
    * `[ ]`   Sources: SW-04 in `docs/research/MVP Application Requirements.md`, whose proof makes a delegated client falsely report possession and verifies the adapter detects the missing or corrupt object; SW-05, SW-07, ST-06, and PR-05 for the holding reason, the archive, the controls, and the obligated holdings, which the family interface states for every concrete; `docs/research/MVP Scope.md`'s statement that a delegated store's accounting is verified, not trusted, by a BLAKE3/Bao authentication path over a random chunk against the ciphertext root; the technical requirements' seed host family subsystem row and the dependency map's `seed-host/rqbit` row; the tech stack's Ciphertext store row, under which the store is whatever the rqbit application uses and the concrete translates; the Swarm transport and seed host milestone's scope for `seed-host/rqbit`; the nodes `seed-host/owned`, `hashing/blake3_bao`, `hashing/factory`, `storage/redb`, `storage/factory`, `encoding/factory`, and `transport/rqbit` for the surfaces this node consumes; the rqbit application's HTTP API as the project's README and `api.rs`, `http_api_types.rs`, and `torrent_state/stats.rs` state it
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `take_custody` of a root not yet held asks the application to add the torrent named by the offered locator's magnet link with the offered peers as its initial peers, waits under the call's deadline until the application reports the torrent finished, refuses an application that reports an error, a torrent that is not one file, or a file whose length differs from the offered content length, and then records the object and its outboard in one committed transaction
    * `[ ]`   Functional: taking a root that is already held calls the application not at all and keeps the stronger reason of the stored and the offered one, so an obligation is never lowered by a voluntary request; the concrete declares capacity enforcement `ReportedOnly`, so `take_custody` evicts nothing and never returns `CapacityExceeded`
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `held_and_served` for a held root draws the chunk index from the caller's uniform and the stored content length, extracts that chunk's authentication path from the stored outboard, reads exactly that chunk's bytes from the application with an HTTP range request, verifies chunk and path against the root, and reports whether the application says the torrent is live; a root the index does not hold is refused as not held, and a chunk that fails verification is refused with the verifier's refusal whole
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `release` of a voluntary holding asks the application to delete the torrent and its files and removes the index entry; an obligated holding is refused by `release` and by a seeding choice of do-not-seed
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `set_seeding_choice` and `reconcile_serving` pause the torrent in the application when the choice is do-not-seed or the host's conditions forbid serving, and resume it otherwise, under the same controls the family configuration carries for every concrete
    * `[ ]`   Functional: `capacity_and_usage` reports the configured capacity and the bytes held by reason, and `enumerate` reports every held object in ascending root order
    * `[ ]`   Functional: the concrete declares its identifier, the holding reasons it distinguishes, `ReportedOnly` capacity enforcement, its adapter version, and the interface version it implements, readable before any instance exists
    * `[ ]`   Non-functional: no filesystem is touched; the application is reached only through the HTTP client the concrete owns; the index records pass through the encoding family and are decoded as untrusted bytes; every response body is decoded as untrusted bytes with only the fields the concrete reads declared; the endpoint, the index table name, and the poll interval are values the caller hands; no secret-typed value is held

  * `[ ]`   `role`
    * `[ ]`   Adapter: the seed host family's second concrete, beside `seed-host/owned`; it implements `ISeedHostAdapter` and consumes the family's interface, holding types, host conditions interface, declaration, and mock, which `seed-host/owned` authored
    * `[ ]`   Declares the concrete's own configuration, record and its description, response types, method errors, and case runner's types in `rqbit/interface.rs`; adds to the family's `factory/interface.rs` only the identifier variant and the error-union variants that carry this concrete's errors
    * `[ ]`   Does not touch a filesystem, hold bytes of an object, or run a BitTorrent engine; the application does
    * `[ ]`   Does not read the clock, the battery, the meter, or the CPU; the host conditions source the caller hands does
    * `[ ]`   Does not authenticate to the application; an endpoint that requires credentials is outside this node
    * `[ ]`   Does not select a concrete or own the factory function; `seed-host/factory` does, and carries the commit
